========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Hi, yeah, nice to meet you too. Um, so I'm here because... well, honestly, I'm just getting really scared about my job. I work in video production—I do a mix of camera operating and editing, mostly editing lately—and it's a really fast-paced industry, you know? You always have to be on your toes and everything is on a tight deadline. But what finally scared me into coming in to see you is that I just keep making these really, really dumb technical mistakes. 

And the thing is, I absolutely know better. I have a degree in this, I've been doing it for a long time, so it's not like I don't know the workflow or how the software works. But I'll just... I don't know, I'll do things like label the camera cards wrong after a long shoot, which is just a huge nightmare for media management. Or I'll be in the edit bay and I'll completely miss a timecode note from the director or the producer. Like, they'll leave a specific note for a cut and I just blow right past it somehow. 

But the absolute worst, the one that really made me pick up the phone and make this appointment, was the other day. I exported a final client cut—and I swear to you I checked it, I really thought I looked over the whole timeline—and I sent it off, and it had the completely wrong lower-third graphic on the screen. Like, the wrong name and title for the person talking. Even after I checked it! It's just... it's so embarrassing, and I'm terrified I'm going to get fired or lose clients if I keep screwing up these basic, fundamental things that I know how to do. I just don't understand why I keep missing them.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Oh, gosh, yeah. I mean, school was... it was definitely an experience. If you ask my mom about it, she’ll probably just sigh, because it was always something. 

Even way back in elementary school, like second or third grade, I remember my teachers were constantly—and I mean constantly—moving my desk around the room. It was like a running joke, except it wasn't really funny. First I’d be in the normal clusters with the other kids, then they’d move me to the very front right next to the chalkboard so the teacher could keep a close eye on me, and then eventually they’d just isolate my desk in a corner by the reading rug. 

And it was because I just... I couldn't stay put. I was always out of my seat. I wouldn't even realize I was standing up half the time! I’d just pop up to go look at the class pet, or I'd wander over to the window, or I'd just be standing next to my chair for absolutely no reason while we were supposed to be doing a worksheet. 

And the talking... oh man. I was notorious for blurting out answers. The teacher would ask the class a question, and if I knew it—or even if I just thought I knew it—I would just shout it out before she even finished the sentence. Raising my hand just didn't even compute in the moment. I was also just constantly bothering my classmates. But the thing is, I really never meant to be annoying or disruptive! I would just start talking to the kid next to me, or I'd reach over and grab their eraser, or I'd be tapping on their desk, and I honestly wouldn't even realize I was doing it until the teacher snapped my name and told me to stop bothering everyone. It was like my body and my mouth were just moving a hundred miles an hour before my brain could even catch up and say, "Hey, wait, we're in the middle of quiet reading time." So, yeah, I definitely had a lot of trouble with that kind of stuff when I was little.

Um, outside of work? Honestly, I don't... I mean, I don't really think so. Or at least, nothing that really stands out or feels like a huge problem the way it does at work. 

I mean, my daily life outside the studio is mostly just me and my dog, Buster. He's a golden retriever mix, completely goofy, and he takes up a lot of my free time. We go on these long walks around the neighborhood, or sometimes I'll take him out to the reservoir if the weather's nice, and I don't really have to hyper-focus on anything when I'm doing that, you know? It's just walking. And around the house, I mean, I guess I'm a little messy sometimes. Like, I'll leave my mail on the kitchen counter for a week before I actually open it, or I might start folding laundry and then just leave the basket sitting on the couch for two days because I got distracted by a podcast or something. 

But I feel like everyone does that, right? That's just normal life stuff. It's not like I'm leaving the stove on or forgetting to pay my rent or anything catastrophic. I have a pretty good system for my bills—I put everything on autopay a few years ago because I just didn't want to deal with the hassle of remembering due dates every single month. It just seemed like a smart thing to do, not really a symptom of anything. 

And socially, I don't really have issues with attention either. If I'm out with friends, getting drinks or catching a movie, I'm fully there. I'm engaged. I don't feel like I'm zoning out when people are talking to me, or making silly mistakes that ruin the night. 

So, yeah, it really just feels like this intense, overwhelming problem when I'm sitting in the edit bay or on set. It's like the pressure of the job just magnifies everything, but when I go home and I'm just living my regular life, I don't really notice those same kinds of careless mistakes causing any real trouble for me. It's really just the work stuff that has me so freaked out.

It was like my body and my mouth were just moving a hundred miles an hour before my brain could even catch up and say, \"Hey, wait, we're in the middle of quiet reading time.\" So, yeah, I definitely had a lot of trouble with that kind of stuff when I was little.\n\nDoctor: Outside of work, do these kinds of attention mistakes also cause problems in your daily life?\n\nPatient: Um, outside of work? Honestly, I don't... I mean, I don't really think so. Or at least, nothing that really stands out or feels like a huge problem the way it does at work. \n\nI mean, my daily life outside the studio is mostly just me and my dog, Buster. He's a golden retriever mix, completely goofy, and he takes up a lot of my free time. We go on these long walks around the neighborhood, or sometimes I'll take him out to the reservoir if the weather's nice, and I don't really have to hyper-focus on anything when I'm doing that, you know? It's just walking. And around the house, I mean, I guess I'm a little messy sometimes. Like, I'll leave my mail on the kitchen counter for a week before I actually open it, or I might start folding laundry and then just leave the basket sitting on the couch for two days because I got distracted by a podcast or something. \n\nBut I feel like everyone does that, right? That's just normal life stuff. It's not like I'm leaving the stove on or forgetting to pay my rent or anything catastrophic. I have a pretty good system for my bills—I put everything on autopay a few years ago because I just didn't want to deal with the hassle of remembering due dates every single month. It just seemed like a smart thing to do, not really a symptom of anything. \n\nAnd socially, I don't really have issues with attention either. If I'm out with friends, getting drinks or catching a movie, I'm fully there. I'm engaged. I don't feel like I'm zoning out when people are talking to me, or making silly mistakes that ruin the night. \n\nSo, yeah, it really just feels like this intense, overwhelming problem when I'm sitting in the edit bay or on set. It's like the pressure of the job just magnifies everything, but when I go home and I'm just living my regular life, I don't really notice those same kinds of careless mistakes causing any real trouble for me. It's really just the work stuff that has me so freaked out.\n\n[Last Question]\nBesides work, do you also have trouble staying organized or following through on tasks in other parts of your life?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Female / 38 / television_video_or_film_camera_operator_or_editor / bachelors\n- severity: Moderate\n\n[Targeted Info]\nNone\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Um, organized? No, I really don't think so. I mean, like I was saying before with the laundry and the mail, I can be a little casual about chores, but I wouldn't say I have trouble staying organized in a way that actually matters. Like, my apartment isn't a disaster zone or anything. I actually have a pretty good system for most things. 

Take Buster’s stuff, for example. I have this whole setup right by the front door—there’s a specific little woven bin for his paw wipes because he gets super muddy in the yard, and then I have these three separate command hooks on the wall. One is for his everyday leash, one is for the long twenty-foot lead we use when we go out to the reservoir so he can swim, and one is for this light-up reflective harness thing we use if we're walking at night. And I always put them right back on the hooks. It's just muscle memory at this point, so I always know exactly where they are.

Like, just this past weekend, I realized I desperately needed to repot my monstera plant. I don't feel like I'm zoning out when people are talking to me, or making silly mistakes that ruin the night. \n\nSo, yeah, it really just feels like this intense, overwhelming problem when I'm sitting in the edit bay or on set. I have this whole setup right by the front door—there’s a specific little woven bin for his paw wipes because he gets super muddy in the yard, and then I have these three separate command hooks on the wall. So Saturday morning, I got up, I walked down\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen you're working, do you spend a lot of time worrying or feeling tense?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Worrying or feeling tense? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm a particularly anxious person, you know? Like, I don't sit there at my editing bay chewing my fingernails off, agonizing over every single cut, or worrying that I'm going to get fired. It's not like that. 

It's more just... frustration, I guess? Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been doing this for over a decade. I know the software inside and out, I know pacing, I know how to tell a story visually. But then I'll be sitting there, and I just... I can't stay in the zone. I'll be trying to sync up audio for a multi-cam shoot, which is tedious but totally straightforward, and suddenly I'm spinning around in my chair, or I'm getting up to grab my fourth coffee of the afternoon, or I'm tapping my foot so fast the whole desk shakes. 

So I don't feel tense like I'm scared or worried about something bad happening. It's more of a physical tension, if that makes sense? Like this buzzing energy under my skin that just wants me to move, move, move, even though I need to sit still and look at these two monitors for the next six hours. And then I get annoyed with myself because I'm making stupid mistakes—like leaving a one-frame black gap between clips, or totally missing a continuity error because I was looking out the window at a pigeon on the fire escape instead of watching the playback. 

But it's not worry. I'm not sitting there thinking, "Oh my god, what if the director hates this?" or "What if I'm a terrible editor?" It's just this constant, exhausting battle of trying to force myself to stay glued to the chair and actually focus on the timeline instead of literally anything else in the room. So, yeah, no real worrying, just... a lot of restless energy, I guess.

Like, my apartment isn't a disaster zone or anything. I actually have a pretty good system for most things. \n\nTake Buster’s stuff, for example. Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge. It's just walking. And around the house, I mean, I guess I'm a little messy sometimes. It just seemed like a smart thing to do, not really a symptom of anything. \n\nAnd socially, I don't really have issues with attention either. I don't feel like I'm zoning out when people are talking to me, or making silly mistakes that ruin the night. \n\nSo, yeah, it really just feels like this intense, overwhelming problem when I'm sitting in the edit bay or on set. Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nWorrying or feeling tense? Um... no, not really. I mean, I don't think I'm a particularly anxious person, you know? Like, I don't sit there at my editing bay chewing my fingernails off, agonizing over every single cut, or worrying that I'm going to get fired. It's not like that. \n\nIt's more just... frustration, I guess? Like, I know I'm good at my job. I've been doing this for over a decade. I know the software inside and out, I know pacing, I know how to tell a story visually. But then I'll be sitting there, and I just... I can't stay in the zone. I'll be trying to sync up audio for a multi-cam shoot, which is tedious but totally straightforward, and suddenly I'm spinning around in my chair, or I'm getting up to grab my fourth coffee of the afternoon, or I'm tapping my foot so fast the whole desk shakes. \n\nSo I don't feel tense like I'm scared or worried about something bad happening. It's more of a physical tension, if that makes sense? Like this buzzing energy under my skin that just wants me to move, move, move, even though I need to sit still and look at these two monitors for the next six hours. And then I get annoyed with myself because I'm making stupid mistakes—like leaving a one-frame black gap between clips, or totally missing a continuity error because I was looking out the window at a pigeon on the fire escape instead of watching the playback. \n\nBut it's not worry. I'm not sitting there thinking, \"Oh my god, what if the director hates this?\" or \"What if I'm a terrible editor?\" It's just this constant, exhausting battle of trying to force myself to stay glued to the chair and actually focus on the timeline instead of literally anything else in the room. So, yeah, no real worrying, just... a lot of restless energy, I guess.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
